[Director 指令] **补充修正（D041 延伸）：paper_framing.md formal definition 方向错误**

paper_framing.md 中 formal definition 写的是 `reachable_G(x,q)`（选结构可达文件），但代码和 method_v1.md 都是选结构**不可达**文件。修正 framing 的 formal definition：`reachable_G(x,q)` → `¬reachable_G(x,q)` 或等价表述 `x ∉ R_G(q)`。

这个和 D041 的 Worker 如果还在处理中可以一并修，否则单独派。确保 paper_framing.md 内部一致：Three-Strategy 描述（已正确）和 formal definition（需修正）对齐。

Automated behaviors (\"from now on when X\", \"each time X\", \"whenever X\", \"before/after X\") require hooks configured in settings.json - the harness executes these, not Claude, so memory/preferences cannot fulfill them. Also use for: permissions (\"allow X\", \"add permission\", \"move permission to\"), env vars (\"set X=Y\"), hook troubleshooting, or any changes to settings.json/settings.local.json files. Examples: \"allow npm commands\", \"add bq permission to global settings\", \"move permission to user settings\", \"set DEBUG=true\", \"when claude stops show X\". For simple settings like theme/model, suggest the /config command.\n- keybindings-help: Use when the user wants to customize keyboard shortcuts, rebind keys, add chord bindings, or modify ~/.claude/keybindings.json. Examples: \"rebind ctrl+s\", \"add a chord shortcut\", \"change the submit key\", \"customize keybindings\".\n- simplify: Review changed code for reuse, quality, and efficiency, then fix any issues found.\n- fewer-permission-prompts: Scan your transcripts for common read-only Bash and MCP tool calls, then add a prioritized allowlist to project .claude/settings.json to reduce permission prompts.\n- loop: Run a prompt or slash command on a recurring interval (e.g. /loop 5m /foo). Omit the interval to let the model self-pace. - When the user wants to set up a recurring task, poll for status, or run something repeatedly on an interval (e.g. \"check the deploy every 5 minutes\", \"keep running /babysit-prs\"). Do NOT invoke for one-off tasks.\n- schedule: Create, update, list, or run scheduled remote agents (routines) that execute on a cron schedule. - When the user wants to schedule a recurring remote agent, set up automated tasks, create a cron job for Claude Code, or manage their scheduled agents/routines. Also use when the user wants a one-time scheduled run (\"run this once at 3pm\", \"remind me to check X tomorrow\").\n- claude-api: Build, debug, and optimize Claude API / Anthropic SDK apps. Apps built with this skill should include prompt caching. {"result":"{\"success\": true, \"data\": {\"path\": \"/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_5/projects/structfuse-context/paper_framing.md\", \"content\": \"# Paper Framing — StructFuse\\n\\n## Target Venue\\n- Primary: EMNLP 2026 Main\\n- Deadline: ARR May 25, 2026\\n\\n## Core Claims (3)\\n\\n1. **Simple static embedding-distance mining suffices for contrastive code retrieval, challenging the assumption that expensive gradient-based hard negative methods are necessary.** CoCoHaNeRe (ACM TOSEM 2025) argues non-differentiable embedding negatives are \\\"slow and ineffective\\\" compared to gradient-based methods — our results show that in structural code retrieval, simple embedding-distance mining yields +1.6–2.0pp R@1 over random baselines across two decoder-only encoders with distinct pretraining corpora and representation geometries (Qwen2.5-Coder-3B, StarCoder2-3B), without requiring gradient-based mechanisms.\\n\\n2. **[Exploratory observation] Structure-grounded (dataflow) negatives interact with encoder representation geometry, but do not consistently outperform embedding mining.** Pilot data shows dataflow < embedding on both encoders (Qwen: -1.2pp, StarCoder: -1.0pp R@1 vs embedding). The interaction pattern — Qwen (saturated, pos_sim > 0.98) shows smaller gap than StarCoder (healthy, pos_sim ~ 0.80) — suggests representation geometry mediates strategy effectiveness. neg_difficulty margin analysis (+17% Qwen, +28% StarCoder) provides mechanistic evidence for this interaction. Pending: multi-seed confirmation + plan_005 stratified analysis + plan_015 dynamic mining control. This is reported as an observation with mechanistic hypothesis, not an established finding.\\n\\n3. **Scope-bounded inter-procedural data-flow analysis via CodeQL provides a practical pipeline for computing structural connectivity at scale.** 0% timeout, 78% connected-not-gold rate (Z%), 123/131 repos with output at full scale. [Dream [M][W] flag: this is supporting methodology, not a primary research contribution. Drop speculative generalization claims (molecular networks, etc.) — no evidence. Frame as practical tool that enables the study, not a standalone contribution.]\\n\\n## Story Spine\\n\\n- **Problem**: Repo-level code repair requires selecting relevant files from large codebases. Current retrievers rely on text similarity alone, but lexical similarity ≠ structural relevance. Hard negative mining is the standard approach to improve contrastive retrievers, yet the field lacks systematic understanding of what negative selection strategies work for code and why.\\n\\n- **Existing approaches & gaps**:\\n  - Text-based retrieval (SweRank, SWE-Fixer): blind to structural dependencies\\n  - Graph-guided at inference (LocAgent, RANGER, CodexGraph): structural graphs at inference only, no training signal\\n  - ARISE: data-flow but intra-procedural only (no call boundary crossing), runtime tool calls only\\n  - CoCoHaNeRe (ACM TOSEM 2025): argues gradient-based hard negative methods outperform non-differentiable embedding negatives — but does not test simple static embedding-distance mining in structural code retrieval settings\\n  - Gap: no systematic study comparing negative mining strategies with proper controls, and no mechanistic explanation for when/why each strategy works\\n\\n- **Our contribution**: A systematic analysis of hard negative mining strategies for contrastive code retrieval. We (1) show that simple static embedding-distance mining suffices, challenging the assumption that expensive gradient-based methods are necessary (CoCoHaNeRe's premise), (2) [exploratory] observe that structure-grounded (dataflow) negatives interact with encoder representation geometry and provide mechanistic analysis of when/why different strategies yield different outcomes, and (3) validate findings on downstream SWE-bench task evaluation.\\n\\n- **Headline result template**: \\\"Simple static embedding-distance mining consistently improves code retrieval (+1.6–2.0pp R@1, 2 encoders), challenging the need for expensive gradient-based hard negative methods. Structure-grounded (dataflow) negatives show encoder-dependent interaction patterns — representation geometry analysis via negative difficulty provides a candidate mechanistic explanation for when different mining strategies are effective.\\\"\\n\\n## Core Insight\\n\\nOur pilot data suggests that the effectiveness of hard negative mining strategies may depend on the encoder's representation geometry — a hypothesis derived from two encoders and requiring broader validation. The three strategies explore different dimensions of difficulty: (1) random — no signal baseline; (2) embedding-distance — semantic similarity via pretrained encoder cosine similarity; (3) dataflow-aware — structural filtering (CodeQL-connected files) + lexical similarity (BM25 top-K ranking). When the pretrained space is saturated (high pos_sim → low margin between positive and negative), the encoder needs harder negatives from an orthogonal signal dimension (structure + lexical). When the space has healthy separation, embedding-distance mining alone captures sufficient difficulty gradient. This encoder-strategy interaction, if confirmed, could help explain contradictory findings in prior work.\\n\\n## Dual-Pillar Motivation\\n\\n1. **Primary — Systematic analysis challenging gradient-based assumptions**: Prior work (CoCoHaNeRe, ACM TOSEM 2025) assumes expensive gradient-based hard negative methods are necessary for effective code search. Our controlled comparison shows simple static embedding-distance mining suffices in structural code retrieval, isolating the effect of each strategy.\\n2. **Secondary — Mechanistic explanation via representation geometry**: neg_difficulty analysis (margin distribution × strategy × encoder) provides explanatory depth beyond empirical comparison, offering a candidate heuristic (pos_sim threshold) that, if validated on more encoders, could help practitioners select strategies without exhaustive experiments.\\n\\n## Formal Definition\\n\\nGiven retrieval domain D, structural graph G, query q:\\n**structure-grounded hard negative** = argmax_{x ∈ D : reachable_G(x,q) ∧ x ∉ gold(q)} BM25(q, x)\\n\\nThe structural graph is externally fixed and does not update with the encoder (drift-immune). Negatives are selected from CodeQL-connected files (structural filtering), ranked by BM25 (lexical similarity).\\n\\n## Title Options\\n\\n- Primary: \\\"What Makes Hard Negatives Work for Code Retrieval? A Systematic Study of Mining Strategies and Representation Geometry\\\"\\n- Alternative: \\\"Beyond Gradient-Based Mining: Static Embedding Negatives Suffice for Contrastive Code Retrieval\\\" [contingent: activate only if M2 dataflow conditional benefit confirmed across encoders]\\n- Backup: \\\"Simple Embedding Mining Suffices: Challenging Gradient-Based Assumptions in Hard Negative Mining for Code\\\"\\n\\n## Venue Fit\\n\\n1. **EMNLP Main fit**: Empirical analysis + mechanistic insight papers are a strong EMNLP archetype (cf. \\\"Do Language Models...\\\" papers). Contribution is understanding, not just a new method.\\n2. **NLP audience**: Frame as contrastive learning methodology with code as instantiation. Representation geometry analysis generalizes beyond code.\\n3. **Against \\\"incremental\\\" criticism**: Paper challenges a prevalent assumption + provides mechanistic explanation for strategy × encoder interaction + validates on downstream task. Three contributions, not one marginal improvement.\\n\\n## Anticipated Reviewer Concerns\\n\\n| Concern | Response | Evidence |\\n|---------|----------|----------|\\n| Only 2 encoders — can you generalize? | n=2 is minimum for interaction claim; we frame as observation with mechanism, not universal law; two decoder-only encoders with distinct pretraining (Fill vs FIM) and representation geometry (pos_sim 0.99 vs 0.80) provide meaningful variation | plan_013 (done), m2_full runs |\\n| BM25 vs true embedding negatives? | Run BM25 negatives to empirically compare with embedding-distance mining, grounding the comparison against CoCoHaNeRe's gradient-based premise | plan_018 |\\n| Pool size sensitivity? | Evaluate at pool {50, 100, 200, 500} to confirm strategy rankings hold at realistic sizes | plan_019 |\\n| Stratified by structural data availability? | Report per-stratum R@1 for 34% with structural data vs 66% fallback | plan_005 (extended) |\\n| Temperature regime specificity | Ablate τ ∈ {0.05, 0.1, 0.2} | plan_014 |\\n| No downstream task evaluation | SWE-bench Lite end-to-end with LLM agent | plan_003 |\\n| Static vs dynamic mining confound | Clarify mining is static; add dynamic baseline | plan_015 |\\n| CodeQL scalability at larger scale | 0% timeout, 78% Z%, 123 repos; m1_reextract_v2 adds more | plan_006 (done) |\\n| Why not compare inference-time graph methods? | Complementary: they navigate at inference, we mine at training. Discuss relationship. | plan_017 |\\n| Single benchmark | Add RepoBench-R cross-file retrieval | plan_004 |\\n| Statistical significance | Bootstrap 95% CI for all comparisons; full M2 = 9 seeds × 3 strategies × 2 encoders | plan_016, m2_full |\\n| \\\"Complementary signal\\\" overstatement | Temper: \\\"conditionally beneficial\\\" not \\\"always complementary\\\" | Writing discipline |\\n| We (1) show that simple static embedding-distance mining suffices, challenging the assumption that expensive gradient-based methods are necessary (CoCoHaNeRe's premise), (2) [exploratory] observe that structure-grounded (dataflow) negatives interact with encoder representation geometry and provide mechanistic analysis of when/why different strategies yield different outcomes, and (3) validate findings on downstream SWE-bench task evaluation.\\n\\n- **Headline result template**: \\\"Simple static embedding-distance mining consistently improves code retrieval (+1.6–2.0pp R@1, 2 encoders), challenging the need for expensive gradient-based hard negative methods. Structure-grounded (dataflow) negatives show encoder-dependent interaction patterns — representation geometry analysis via negative difficulty provides a candidate mechanistic explanation for when different mining strategies are effective.\\\"\\n\\n## Core Insight\\n\\nOur pilot data suggests that the effectiveness of hard negative mining strategies may depend on the encoder's representation geometry — a hypothesis derived from two encoders and requiring broader validation. The three strategies explore different dimensions of difficulty: (1) random — no signal baseline; (2) embedding-distance — semantic similarity via pretrained encoder cosine similarity; (3) dataflow-aware — structural filtering (CodeQL-connected files) + lexical similarity (BM25 top-K ranking). When the pretrained space is saturated (high pos_sim → low margin between positive and negative), the encoder needs harder negatives from an orthogonal signal dimension (structure + lexical). When the space has healthy separation, embedding-distance mining alone captures sufficient difficulty gradient. This encoder-strategy interaction, if confirmed, could help explain contradictory findings in prior work.\\n\\n## Dual-Pillar Motivation\\n\\n1. **Primary — Systematic analysis challenging gradient-based assumptions**: Prior work (CoCoHaNeRe, ACM TOSEM 2025) assumes expensive gradient-based hard negative methods are necessary for effective code search. Our controlled comparison shows simple static embedding-distance mining suffices in structural code retrieval, isolating the effect of each strategy.\\n2. **Secondary — Mechanistic explanation via representation geometry**: neg_difficulty analysis (margin distribution × strategy × encoder) provides explanatory depth beyond empirical comparison, offering a candidate heuristic (pos_sim threshold) that, if validated on more encoders, could help practitioners select strategies without exhaustive experiments.\\n\\n## Formal Definition\\n\\nGiven retrieval domain D, structural graph G, query q:\\n**structure-grounded hard negative** = argmax_{x ∈ D : reachable_G(x,q) ∧ x ∉ gold(q)} BM25(q, x)\\n\\nThe structural graph is externally fixed and does not update with the encoder (drift-immune). Negatives are selected from CodeQL-connected files (structural filtering), ranked by BM25 (lexical similarity).\\n\\n## Title Options\\n\\n- Primary: \\\"What Makes Hard Negatives Work for Code Retrieval? A Systematic Study of Mining Strategies and Representation Geometry\\\"\\n- Alternative: \\\"Beyond Gradient-Based Mining: Static Embedding Negatives Suffice for Contrastive Code Retrieval\\\" [contingent: activate only if M2 dataflow conditional benefit confirmed across encoders]\\n- Backup: \\\"Simple Embedding Mining Suffices: Challenging Gradient-Based Assumptions in Hard Negative Mining for Code\\\"\\n\\n## Venue Fit\\n\\n1. **EMNLP Main fit**: Empirical analysis + mechanistic insight papers are a strong EMNLP archetype (cf. \\\"Do Language Models...\\\" papers). Contribution is understanding, not just a new method.\\n2. **NLP audience**: Frame as contrastive learning methodology with code as instantiation. Representation geometry analysis generalizes beyond code.\\n3. **Against \\\"incremental\\\" criticism**: Paper challenges a prevalent assumption + provides mechanistic explanation for strategy × encoder interaction + validates on downstream task. Three contributions, not one marginal improvement.\\n\\n## Anticipated Reviewer Concerns\\n\\n| Concern | Response | Evidence |\\n|---------|----------|----------|\\n| Only 2 encoders — can you generalize? | n=2 is minimum for interaction claim; we frame as observation with mechanism, not universal law; two decoder-only encoders with distinct pretraining (Fill vs FIM) and representation geometry (pos_sim 0.99 vs 0.80) provide meaningful variation | plan_013 (done), m2_full runs |\\n| BM25 vs true embedding negatives? | Run BM25 negatives to empirically compare with embedding-distance mining, grounding the comparison against CoCoHaNeRe's gradient-based premise | plan_018 |\\n| Pool size sensitivity? | Evaluate at pool {50, 100, 200, 500} to confirm strategy rankings hold at realistic sizes | plan_019 |\\n| Stratified by structural data availability? | Report per-stratum R@1 for 34% with structural data vs 66% fallback | plan_005 (extended) |\\n| Temperature regime specificity | Ablate τ ∈ {0.05, 0.1, 0.2} | plan_014 |\\n| No downstream task evaluation | SWE-bench Lite end-to-end with LLM agent | plan_003 |\\n| Static vs dynamic mining confound | Clarify mining is static; add dynamic baseline | plan_015 |\\n| CodeQL scalability at larger scale | 0% timeout, 78% Z%, 123 repos; m1_reextract_v2 adds more | plan_006 (done) |\\n| Why not compare inference-time graph methods? | Complementary: they navigate at inference, we mine at training. Discuss relationship. | plan_017 |\\n| Single benchmark | Add RepoBench-R cross-file retrieval | plan_004 |\\n| Statistical significance | Bootstrap 95% CI for all comparisons; full M2 = 9 seeds × 3 strategies × 2 encoders | plan_016, m2_full |\\n| \\\"Complementary signal\\\" overstatement | Temper: \\\"conditionally beneficial\\\" not \\\"always complementary\\\" | Writing discipline |\\n| Missing related work | RepoFusion, GRACE, GraphCodeBERT, ANCE, curriculum HN mining | Writing task |\\n\\n## Evidence Status\\n\\n### Confirmed findings (basis for claims):\\n- D017: True embedding mining helps (+2.0pp Qwen, +1.6pp StarCoder) — challenges gradient-based necessity assumption\\n- D021/neg_difficulty: Qwen pos_sim=0.9855 (saturated), StarCoder pos_sim=0.7985 (healthy); margin differential explains strategy interaction\\n- m2_embed_verify (pilot): 3-strategy comparison confirms ranking (embedding > random, dataflow conditional)\\n- plan_013 (StarCoder pilot): Confirms cross-encoder pattern — embedding helps both, dataflow helps Qwen only\\n- plan_006: CodeQL scalability confirmed at 123-repo scale\\n\\n### In progress:\\n- m2_full_qwen: random s42 DONE {

  "content": "# Paper Framing — StructFuse\n\n## Target Venue\n- Primary: EMNLP 2026 Main\n- Deadline: ARR May 25, 2026\n\n## Core Claims (3)\n\n1. **Simple static embedding-distance mining suffices for contrastive code retrieval, challenging the assumption that expensive gradient-based hard negative methods are necessary.** CoCoHaNeRe (ACM TOSEM 2025) argues non-differentiable embedding negatives are \"slow and ineffective\" compared to gradient-based methods — our results show that in structural code retrieval, simple embedding-distance mining yields +1.6–2.0pp R@1 over random baselines across two decoder-only encoders with distinct pretraining corpora and representation geometries (Qwen2.5-Coder-3B, StarCoder2-3B), without requiring gradient-based mechanisms.\n\n2. **[Exploratory observation] Structure-grounded (dataflow) negatives interact with encoder representation geometry, but do not consistently outperform embedding mining.** Pilot data shows dataflow < embedding on both encoders (Qwen: -1.2pp, StarCoder: -1.0pp R@1 vs embedding). The interaction pattern — Qwen (saturated, pos_sim > 0.98) shows smaller gap than StarCoder (healthy, pos_sim ~ 0.80) — suggests representation geometry mediates strategy effectiveness. neg_difficulty margin analysis (+17% Qwen, +28% StarCoder) provides mechanistic evidence for this interaction. Pending: multi-seed confirmation + plan_005 stratified analysis + plan_015 dynamic mining control. This is reported as an observation with mechanistic hypothesis, not an established finding.\n\n3. **Scope-bounded inter-procedural data-flow analysis via CodeQL provides a practical pipeline for computing structural connectivity at scale.** 0% timeout, 78% connected-not-gold rate (Z%), 123/131 repos with output at full scale. [Dream [M][W] flag: this is supporting methodology, not a primary research contribution. Drop speculative generalization claims (molecular networks, etc.) — no evidence. Frame as practical tool that enables the study, not a standalone contribution.]\n\n## Story Spine\n\n- **Problem**: Repo-level code repair requires selecting relevant files from large codebases. Current retrievers rely on text similarity alone, but lexical similarity ≠ structural relevance. Hard negative mining is the standard approach to improve contrastive retrievers, yet the field lacks systematic understanding of what negative selection strategies work for code and why.\n\n- **Existing approaches & gaps**:\n  - Text-based retrieval (SweRank, SWE-Fixer): blind to structural dependencies\n  - Graph-guided at inference (LocAgent, RANGER, CodexGraph): structural graphs at inference only, no training signal\n  - ARISE: data-flow but intra-procedural only (no call boundary crossing), runtime tool calls only\n  - CoCoHaNeRe (ACM TOSEM 2025): argues gradient-based hard negative methods outperform non-differentiable embedding negatives — but does not test simple static embedding-distance mining in structural code retrieval settings\n  - Gap: no systematic study comparing negative mining strategies with proper controls, and no mechanistic explanation for when/why each strategy works\n\n- **Our contribution**: A systematic analysis of hard negative mining strategies for contrastive code retrieval. We (1) show that simple static embedding-distance mining suffices, challenging the assumption that expensive gradient-based methods are necessary (CoCoHaNeRe's premise), (2) [exploratory] observe that structure-grounded (dataflow) negatives interact with encoder representation geometry and provide mechanistic analysis of when/why different strategies yield different outcomes, and (3) validate findings on downstream SWE-bench task evaluation.\n\n- **Headline result template**: \"Simple static embedding-distance mining consistently improves code retrieval (+1.6–2.0pp R@1, 2 encoders), challenging the need for expensive gradient-based hard negative methods. Structure-grounded (dataflow) negatives show encoder-dependent interaction patterns — representation geometry analysis via negative difficulty provides a candidate mechanistic explanation for when different mining strategies are effective.\"\n\n## Core Insight\n\nOur pilot data suggests that the effectiveness of hard negative mining strategies may depend on the encoder's representation geometry — a hypothesis derived from two encoders and requiring broader validation. The three strategies explore different dimensions of difficulty: (1) random — no signal baseline; (2) embedding-distance — semantic similarity via pretrained encoder cosine similarity; (3) dataflow-aware — structural filtering (CodeQL-connected files) + lexical similarity (BM25 top-K ranking). When the pretrained space is saturated (high pos_sim → low margin between positive and negative), the encoder needs harder negatives from an orthogonal signal dimension (structure + lexical). When the space has healthy separation, embedding-distance mining alone captures sufficient difficulty gradient. This encoder-strategy interaction, if confirmed, could help explain contradictory findings in prior work.\n\n## Dual-Pillar Motivation\n\n1. **Primary — Systematic analysis challenging gradient-based assumptions**: Prior work (CoCoHaNeRe, ACM TOSEM 2025) assumes expensive gradient-based hard negative methods are necessary for effective code search. Our controlled comparison shows simple static embedding-distance mining suffices in structural code retrieval, isolating the effect of each strategy.\n2. **Secondary — Mechanistic explanation via representation geometry**: neg_difficulty analysis (margin distribution × strategy × encoder) provides explanatory depth beyond empirical comparison, offering a candidate heuristic (pos_sim threshold) that, if validated on more encoders, could help practitioners select strategies without exhaustive experiments.\n\n## Formal Definition\n\nGiven retrieval domain D, structural graph G, query q:\n**structure-grounded hard negative** = argmax_{x ∈ D : ¬reachable_G(x,q) ∧ x ∉ gold(q)} BM25(q, x)\n\nThe structural graph is externally fixed and does not update with the encoder (drift-immune). Negatives are selected from structurally unreachable files (i.e., x ∉ R_G(q), not connected via CodeQL data-flow to the query), ranked by BM25 (lexical similarity).\n\n## Title Options\n\n- Primary: \"What Makes Hard Negatives Work for Code Retrieval? A Systematic Study of Mining Strategies and Representation Geometry\"\n- Alternative: \"Beyond Gradient-Based Mining: Static Embedding Negatives Suffice for Contrastive Code Retrieval\" [contingent: activate only if M2 dataflow conditional benefit confirmed across encoders]\n- Backup: \"Simple Embedding Mining Suffices: Challenging Gradient-Based Assumptions in Hard Negative Mining for Code\"\n\n## Venue Fit\n\n1. **EMNLP Main fit**: Empirical analysis + mechanistic insight papers are a strong EMNLP archetype (cf. \"Do Language Models...\" papers). Contribution is understanding, not just a new method.\n2. **NLP audience**: Frame as contrastive learning methodology with code as instantiation. Representation geometry analysis generalizes beyond code.\n3. **Against \"incremental\" criticism**: Paper challenges a prevalent assumption + provides mechanistic explanation for strategy × encoder interaction + validates on downstream task. Three contributions, not one marginal improvement.\n\n## Anticipated Reviewer Concerns\n\n| Concern | Response | Evidence |\n|---------|----------|----------|\n| Only 2 encoders — can you generalize? | n=2 is minimum for interaction claim; we frame as observation with mechanism, not universal law; two decoder-only encoders with distinct pretraining (Fill vs FIM) and representation geometry (pos_sim 0.99 vs 0.80) provide meaningful variation | plan_013 (done), m2_full runs |\n| BM25 vs true embedding negatives? | Run BM25 negatives to empirically compare with embedding-distance mining, grounding the comparison against CoCoHaNeRe's gradient-based premise | plan_018 |\n| Pool size sensitivity? | Evaluate at pool {50, 100, 200, 500} to confirm strategy rankings hold at realistic sizes | plan_019 |\n| Stratified by structural data availability? | Report per-stratum R@1 for 34% with structural data vs 66% fallback | plan_005 (extended) |\n| Temperature regime specificity | Ablate τ ∈ {0.05, 0.1, 0.2} | plan_014 |\n| No downstream task evaluation | SWE-bench Lite end-to-end with LLM agent | plan_003 |\n| Static vs dynamic mining confound | Clarify mining is static; add dynamic baseline | plan_015 |\n| CodeQL scalability at larger scale | 0% timeout, 78% Z%, 123 repos; m1_reextract_v2 adds more | plan_006 (done) |\n| Why not compare inference-time graph methods? | Complementary: they navigate at inference, we mine at training. Discuss relationship. | plan_017 |\n| Single benchmark | Add RepoBench-R cross-file retrieval | plan_004 |\n| Statistical significance | Bootstrap 95% CI for all comparisons; full M2 = 9 seeds × 3 RUNNING (eval@500 R@1=0.6009). 2/10 runs complete, ~50h remaining.\n- m2_full_starcoder: random s42 DONE (R@1=0.4604, from parent), s43 RUNNING (eval@500 R@1=0.4708). 1/9+ runs complete, ~65-70h Temperature ablation (Dream reviewer concern)\n- plan_004: RepoBench-R second benchmark (with multi-seed CIs)\n- plan_015: Dynamic mining baseline\n- plan_005: Stratified analysis (extended: structural-available vs fallback stratum)\n- plan_007/008/009: Additional ablations\n\n## Contingency (if full M2 results weaken claims)\n\n### All 18 runs confirm pilot pattern: Best case\n- Full statistical power, paper as framed above\n- Target: EMNLP Main\n\n### Dataflow benefit disappears even for Qwen at full scale: Pivot to 2-claim paper\n- Drop Claim 2 to analysis observation\n- Paper
